StrategyQA~\\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. McNemar exact test ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($10{,}000$ resamples) for $n \\geq 200$. Degenerate conditions ($C_0 < 5\\%$: QwQ-32B AIME, Qwen2.5-14B GSM8K) yield $\\Delta = 0$; oracle upper bounds are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $n$ & SC (\\%) & SICA (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\\sim$50--60\\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 50.00 & 51.50 & $+$1.50 & 0.581 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\\\\nMistral-7B$^{3s}$ & FOLIO & 204 & 56.53 & 56.70 & $+$0.16 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 59.31 & 56.86 & $-$2.45 & 0.267 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%)}} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 74.00 & 71.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B$^{4s}$ & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 75.49 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 79.00 & 78.00 & $-$1.00 & 0.625 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 83.33 & 84.80 & $+$1.47 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.83 & 85.67 & $-$0.17 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B (think) & FOLIO & 204 & 86.27 & 86.76 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\looseness=-1 No non-degenerate condition shows a significant SICA effect after FDR control (BH, $m{=}16$). Two show nominally significant degradation: Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($-2.83$\\,pp, raw $p = 0.014$) and Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($-2.50$\\,pp, raw $p = 0.011$); adjusted $p = 0.112$ for both. The intermediate-SC region (50--60\\%) contains the only positive trends, none reaching $p < 0.05$. An aggregate sign test across self-extraction conditions yields 8 positive and 7 negative $\\Delta$ values ($p = 1.00$, two-sided). Three-seed replication on Mistral-7B confirms seed-level noise: two of three seeds produce null or negative $\\Delta$ (mean $+0.16 \\pm 2.70$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:multi-seed}). Four-seed replication on Qwen2.5-14B $\\times$ FOLIO confirms the single-seed pattern: $\\Delta \\in [0.00, -2.01]$\\,pp (mean $-1.24 \\pm 0.86$; Table~\\ref{tab:multi-seed}). TOST equivalence tests \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp confirm 4 equivalent, 2 genuinely different (both negative), 10 underpowered. For the 10 underpowered conditions, our tests cannot rule out effects smaller than 1.86--4.84\\,pp at 80\\% power; the largest study ($n{=}804$) achieves MDE $= 1.86$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). Absence of evidence is not evidence of absence for small effects. Margin sensitivity is robust: at $\\pm 1$\\,pp, 0/16 equivalent; at $\\pm 3$\\,pp, 10/16 equivalent; no margin yields a significant positive effect (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:tost-sensitivity}). We adopt $\\pm 2$\\,pp based on the lower bound of SC literature gains (2--5\\,pp \\citep{wang2023sc}), acknowledging this choice is anchored to SC effect sizes. We trace the failure below.\n\n\n\\looseness=-1 Oracle controls replacing self-extracted constraints with gold annotations confirm the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+17.17$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of this ceiling (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}).\n\n",

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "9535bfc0-2ab4-4bd2-9797-3991675b284e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nAcross six open-source LLMs (7B--32B) on three logical reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find no evidence of improvement (aggregate sign test: 8+/7$-$, $p = 1.00$; none of sixteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect after FDR control), with equivalence established in four conditions, significant degradation in two, and ten remaining underpowered for effects below ${\\sim}$2 percentage points.\nWe trace the failure to \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} (SCB): on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratios ranging from $1.66\\times$ to $4.97\\times$), but a shuffle control shows this bias is structural---it reflects trace-count distributions rather than extraction-specific error ($\\text{BR}_\\text{obs} \\approx \\text{BR}_\\text{null}$, $p > 0.7$). MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\\% of problems, confirming that the formal verification layer adds no information beyond majority voting despite 99\\% of constraints passing Z3 validation.\nCross-model extraction confirms SCB universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$), though frontier models use cross-model rather than self-extraction.\nTwenty-seven training-free remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, confirming a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off.\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\\,pp, $+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+17.17$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of this ceiling.\nCross-architecture NLI verification escapes the bottleneck ($+7.00$\\,pp zero-shot on the weakest generator, $p < 0.001$; $+$13--26\\,pp with domain adaptation extending to generators with SC up to 72\\%), with gain decreasing monotonically with generator capability ($\\rho = 1.0$, $p = 0.003$, $n{=}6$).\nThree zero-cost diagnostics ($K_\\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly classify all 60 tested conditions, giving practitioners an a priori prediction of whether symbolic aggregation will help.\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/figures/gen_capability_gap_scatter.py",
        "content": "\"\"\"Generate capability gap scatter plot for §6.\n\nDeBERTa-MNLI primary (6 pts) + LLaMA-8B (1 pt, distinct marker) + multi-verifier (2 pts)\n+ fine-tuned DeBERTa (2 pts). All gains at w=3.\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy.optimize import curve_fit\nfrom scipy.stats import spearmanr, linregress\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'serif',\n    'font.serif': ['Times New Roman', 'DejaVu Serif'],\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.labelsize': 10,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.08,\n})\n\n# Primary: DeBERTa-MNLI across model x dataset at w=3\ndeberta_pts = [\n    (-36.76, 0.49, 'Qwen3-14B$_{\\\\mathrm{think}}$\\n(FOLIO)'),\n    (-35.29, 0.49, 'Qwen3-14B\\n(FOLIO)'),\n    (-30.83, 0.50, 'Qwen3-14B\\n(PW-D5)'),\n    (-19.00, 0.67, 'Qwen2.5-14B\\n(PW-D5)'),\n    (-6.37,  2.94, 'Mistral-7B\\n(FOLIO)'),\n    (15.83,  7.00, 'Mistral-7B\\n(ProofWriter)'),\n]\n\n# LLaMA point (different model family, separate marker)\nllama_pt = (-17.50, 0.83, 'LLaMA-8B\\n(PW-D5)')\n\n# Secondary: other NLI verifiers on Mistral-7B ProofWriter at w=3\nmulti_nli = [\n    (-1.50, 4.17, 'RoBERTa'),   # 37.67 - 39.17\n    (7.00,  5.17, 'BART'),       # 46.17 - 39.17\n]\n\n# Fine-tuned DeBERTa on ProofWriter OWA (99.67% standalone), w=3\nfinetuned_pts = [\n    (27.17, 11.67, 'LLaMA-8B'),    # 99.67 - 72.50\n    (60.50, 13.00, 'Mistral-7B'),   # 99.67 - 39.17\n]\n\ndx = np.array([p[0] for p in deberta_pts])\ndy = np.array([p[1] for p in deberta_pts])\n\n# DeBERTa-only (excl. LLaMA) stats\nrho_excl, p_excl = spearmanr(dx, dy)\nprint(f\"DeBERTa-only (excl LLaMA) Spearman rho={rho_excl:.3f} (n={len(dx)}, p={p_excl:.4f})\")\n\n# All DeBERTa-verifier points (incl. LLaMA)\ndx_all = np.append(dx, llama_pt[0])\ndy_all = np.append(dy, llama_pt[1])\nrho, p_rho = spearmanr(dx_all, dy_all)\nprint(f\"DeBERTa-all (incl LLaMA) Spearman rho={rho:.3f} (n={len(dx_all)}, p={p_rho:.4f})\")\nslope, intercept, r_value, p_lin, _ = linregress(dx_all, dy_all)\nprint(f\"DeBERTa-all linear: R²={r_value**2:.3f}, p={p_lin:.4f}\")\n\n# Sigmoid with Director's parameters as guide\ndef sigmoid(x, L, k, x0, b):\n    return L / (1 + np.exp(-k * (x - x0))) + b\n\n# Use all points for visual fit (DeBERTa + LLaMA + multi-NLI)\nall_pts = deberta_pts + [llama_pt] + multi_nli\nax_all = np.array([p[0] for p in all_pts])\nay_all = np.array([p[1] for p in all_pts])\n\ntry:\n    popt, _ = curve_fit(sigmoid, ax_all, ay_all,\n                        p0=[3.5, 0.3, 1.0, 0.2],\n                        bounds=([0.5, 0.01, -10, -1], [5.0, 2.0, 15, 2.0]),\n                        maxfev=50000)\n    x_fit = np.linspace(-42, 68, 500)\n    y_sig = sigmoid(x_fit, *popt)\n    ss_res = np.sum((ay_all - sigmoid(ax_all, *popt))**2)\n    ss_tot = np.sum((ay_all - np.mean(ay_all))**2)\n    r2_sig = 1 - ss_res / ss_tot\n    inflection = popt[2]\n    print(f\"Sigmoid ({len(ax_all)}pt): R²={r2_sig:.3f}, inflection={inflection:.1f}pp, L={popt[0]:.2f}, k={popt[1]:.3f}\")\n    has_sigmoid = True\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print(f\"Sigmoid fit failed: {e}\")\n    has_sigmoid = False\n    x_fit = np.linspace(-42, 68, 500)\n\n# Plot\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(3.8, 2.8))\nfig.subplots_adjust(left=0.16, bottom=0.20, right=0.96, top=0.94)\n\n# DeBERTa primary points (Mistral/Qwen generators)\nax.scatter(dx, dy, marker='o', c='#2166ac', s=50, zorder=5,\n           label='DeBERTa-MNLI', edgecolors='white', linewidths=0.5)\n\n# LLaMA generator (different model family)\nax.scatter([llama_pt[0]], [llama_pt[1]], marker='D', c='#e66101', s=50, zorder=5,\n           label='DeBERTa-MNLI (LLaMA)', edgecolors='white', linewidths=0.5)\n\n# Multi-NLI secondary points\nmx = [p[0] for p in multi_nli]\nmy = [p[1] for p in multi_nli]\nml = [p[2] for p in multi_nli]\nax.scatter([mx[0]], [my[0]], marker='s', c='#b2182b', s=40, zorder=5,\n           label='RoBERTa-MNLI', edgecolors='white', linewidths=0.5)\nax.scatter([mx[1]], [my[1]], marker='^', c='#4dac26', s=40, zorder=5,\n           label='BART-MNLI', edgecolors='white', linewidths=0.5)\n\n# Fine-tuned DeBERTa points\nfx = [p[0] for p in finetuned_pts]\nfy = [p[1] for p in finetuned_pts]\nax.scatter(fx, fy, marker='*', c='#7b3294', s=100, zorder=6,\n           label='Fine-tuned DeBERTa', edgecolors='white', linewidths=0.5)\n\n# Sigmoid curve\nif has_sigmoid:\n    ax.plot(x_fit, y_sig, '-', color='#555555', linewidth=1.0, alpha=0.6,\n            label=f'Sigmoid fit')\n\n# Annotations for DeBERTa points\nannot_config = [\n    (-36.76, 0.49, 'Qwen3-14B$_{\\\\mathrm{think}}$\\nFOLIO', (6, -3), 'left'),\n    (-35.29, 0.49, 'Qwen3-14B\\nFOLIO', (6, -15), 'left'),\n    (-30.83, 0.50, 'Qwen3-14B\\nPW-D5', (6, 3), 'left'),\n    (-19.00, 0.67, 'Qwen2.5-14B\\nPW-D5', (6, -8), 'left'),\n    (-6.37,  2.94, 'Mistral-7B\\nFOLIO', (6, -8), 'left'),\n    (15.83,  7.00, 'Mistral-7B\\nPW-D5', (-6, 5), 'right'),\n]\nfor x, y, lbl, offset, ha in annot_config:\n    ax.annotate(lbl, (x, y), textcoords='offset points',\n                xytext=offset, fontsize=5.5, ha=ha, color='#444444',\n                linespacing=0.9)\n\n# LLaMA annotation (distinct color)\nax.annotate('LLaMA-8B\\nPW-D5', (llama_pt[0], llama_pt[1]),\n            textcoords='offset points', xytext=(6, -10), fontsize=5.5,\n            ha='left', color='#e66101', linespacing=0.9)\n\n# Annotations for multi-NLI\nax.annotate('RoBERTa', (mx[0], my[0]), textcoords='offset points',\n            xytext=(5, 5), fontsize=5.5, ha='left', color='#b2182b')\nax.annotate('BART', (mx[1], my[1]), textcoords='offset points',\n            xytext=(5, 5), fontsize=5.5, ha='left', color='#4dac26')\n\n# Annotations for fine-tuned\nax.annotate('LLaMA-8B\\n(fine-tuned)', (fx[0], fy[0]), textcoords='offset points',\n            xytext=(-6, 5), fontsize=5.5, ha='right', color='#7b3294', linespacing=0.9)\nax.annotate('Mistral-7B\\n(fine-tuned)', (fx[1], fy[1]), textcoords='offset points',\n            xytext=(-6, 5), fontsize=5.5, ha='right', color='#7b3294', linespacing=0.9)\n\n# Reference lines\nax.axhline(0, color='black', linewidth=0.3, alpha=0.2)\nax.axvline(0, color='black', linewidth=0.5, alpha=0.25, linestyle=':')\n\n# Region labels\nax.text(-21, 14.0, 'verifier $<$ generator', fontsize=6.5, color='#aaaaaa',\n        ha='center', style='italic')\nax.text(40, 14.0, 'verifier $>$ generator', fontsize=6.5, color='#aaaaaa',\n        ha='center', style='italic')\n\nax.set_xlabel('Capability gap (verifier $-$ generator SC, pp)')\nax.set_ylabel('Combo gain over SC (pp, $w{=}3$)')\nax.set_xlim(-42, 68)\nax.set_ylim(-0.3, 15.0)\nax.legend(loc='lower right', framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='#cccccc',\n          borderpad=0.3, handletextpad=0.4, bbox_to_anchor=(1.0, 0.0))\nax.spines['top'].set_visible(False)\nax.spines['right'].set_visible(False)\n\n# no tight_layout — manual subplots_adjust above\noutdir = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/figures'\nfig.savefig(f'{outdir}/capability_gap_scatter.pdf')\nfig.savefig(f'{outdir}/capability_gap_scatter.png')\nprint(\"Saved to\", outdir)\n",

  "sessionId": "6e0077cd-ef03-4ff5-b72c-7cf0ad14c0f1",
  "content": "[Director 指令] **战略写作重构任务 — 目标 4.0+**\n\n当前状态：Round 14 评分 3.5（Borderline Conference），审稿人明确指出升分路径是 presentation 改进而非新实验。距 DDL 5/25 还有 2 天。\n\n## 核心问题\n\n论文实验覆盖度和统计严谨性已达顶会水准（Soundness 4/5），但 Significance（2.5-3.5/5）和 Presentation 拖了后腿。需要从整体上重新思考写作策略，而非局部修补。\n\n## 审稿人识别的关键问题\n\n1. **信息密度过高**：8 页正文 13 个技术缩写，审稿人认知负担大\n2. **深浅不均**：§3.1 CJT 推导（非原创）占半页，§5.5 Remediation（27 种修复）只有 ~15 行，§7 Discussion 只有 1/3 页\n3. **Pages 3-5 纯文字墙**：Figure 1 在 p2，Table 1 在 p6，中间 3 页无 display item\n4. **Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis 措辞**：Predictions 1-2 是事后总结，只有 Prediction 3 是真正的 prediction。需要重定位为 observations + testable prediction\n5. **Cross-architecture escape 结论过强**：只有 Mistral-7B 显著，需软化为 preliminary evidence\n6. **Abstract power caveat 不足**：10/16 条件 underpowered\n7. **惊喜度不够**：核心发现在 Huang et al. 之后可预见性高。需要突出 Z3 formal solver 场景的特殊性 — \"即使加了外部求解器，extraction 偏差仍使验证层失效\"\n\n## 你的任务\n\n**从整体反思**论文的写作策略，给出一个完整的重构方案。不要局部修补，要考虑：\n\n1. **8 页空间的最优分配**：什么该留在正文？什么该移到附录？哪些内容值得深入展开？\n2. **叙事重心**：当前论文试图在 8 页展示所有东西，结果每个都\"点到即止\"。选 3-4 个最强贡献深入展开，其余精简或移附录。\n3. **惊喜度提升**：如何让 framing 更有冲击力？\"Z3 formal solver 也被 extraction bias 击败\"比\"又一个 self-correction 失败\"更有新闻价值。\n4. **可视化策略**：Pages 3-5 的文字墙如何打破？是否需要新的 figure/table？\n5. **术语瘦身**：13 个缩写哪些是必须的？哪些可以用自然语言替代？\n6. **Mirage Hypothesis 重构**：怎么把 observations 和 predictions 分开，让框架更有预测力？\n7. **Discussion 扩展**：三个零成本诊断 60/60 分类是很强的实用贡献，如何充分展开让审稿人 get 到价值？\n\n**如果你的方案需要补充实验或分析**（比如新的 figure 数据、新的统计量、新的 baseline 对比），直接在方案中列出，我会让 Agent 全力支持。\n\n请先输出 writing_plan.yaml 提交给我审批，再执行。投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25。引用需要验证。"
}
```

[Director 指令] **战略写作重构任务 — 目标 4.0+**

当前状态：Round 14 评分 3.5（Borderline Conference），审稿人明确指出升分路径是 presentation 改进而非新实验。距 DDL 5/25 还有 2 天。

## 核心问题

论文实验覆盖度和统计严谨性已达顶会水准（Soundness 4/5），但 Significance（2.5-3.5/5）和 Presentation 拖了后腿。需要从整体上重新思考写作策略，而非局部修补。

## 审稿人识别的关键问题

1. **信息密度过高**：8 页正文 13 个技术缩写，审稿人认知负担大
2. **深浅不均**：§3.1 CJT 推导（非原创）占半页，§5.5 Remediation（27 种修复）只有 ~15 行，§7 Discussion 只有 1/3 页
3. **Pages 3-5 纯文字墙**：Figure 1 在 p2，Table 1 在 p6，中间 3 页无 display item
4. **Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis 措辞**：Predictions 1-2 是事后总结，只有 Prediction 3 是真正的 prediction。需要重定位为 observations + testable prediction
5. **Cross-architecture escape 结论过强**：只有 Mistral-7B 显著，需软化为 preliminary evidence
6. **Abstract power caveat 不足**：10/16 条件 underpowered
7. **惊喜度不够**：核心发现在 Huang et al. 之后可预见性高。需要突出 Z3 formal solver 场景的特殊性 — "即使加了外部求解器，extraction 偏差仍使验证层失效"

## 你的任务

**从整体反思**论文的写作策略，给出一个完整的重构方案。不要局部修补，要考虑：

1. **8 页空间的最优分配**：什么该留在正文？什么该移到附录？哪些内容值得深入展开？
2. **叙事重心**：当前论文试图在 8 页展示所有东西，结果每个都"点到即止"。选 3-4 个最强贡献深入展开，其余精简或移附录。
3. **惊喜度提升**：如何让 framing 更有冲击力？"Z3 formal solver 也被 extraction bias 击败"比"又一个 self-correction 失败"更有新闻价值。
4. **可视化策略**：Pages 3-5 的文字墙如何打破？是否需要新的 figure/table？
5. **术语瘦身**：13 个缩写哪些是必须的？哪些可以用自然语言替代？
6. **Mirage Hypothesis 重构**：怎么把 observations 和 predictions 分开，让框架更有预测力？
7. **Discussion 扩展**：三个零成本诊断 60/60 分类是很强的实用贡献，如何充分展开让审稿人 get 到价值？

**如果你的方案需要补充实验或分析**（比如新的 figure 数据、新的统计量、新的 baseline 对比），直接在方案中列出，我会让 Agent 全力支持。

请先输出 writing_plan.yaml 提交给我审批，再执行。投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25。引用需要验证。

47	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
48	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
49	\label{sec:effective-k}
50	
51	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$. Two conditions are jointly necessary for any positive trend: sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}).
52	
53	This correlation manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.
54	
55	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts logical constraints consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.85\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\times}^{\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
56

SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$, 4 traces each.}
61	\label{tab:independence}

86	
87	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
88	\label{sec:process-kappa}
89	
90	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence limitation: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
91	\begin{equation}
92	\label{eq:process-sim}
93	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
94	\end{equation}
95	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
100	\vspace{-2mm}
101	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}
102	\label{fig:process-kappa}
103	\end{figure}
104	
105	
106	\subsection{Regime-Dependent Effects}
107	\label{sec:main-results}
108	\label{sec:diagnostics}
109	
110	SICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime (Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter}).
111	
112	\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\sim$50--60\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\,pp. The default-seed and seed-456 runs share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), suggesting that seed-level constraint quality, not SC level alone, influences the effect.
113	
114	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ 60\%).} All nine Type~A conditions produce no significant positive effects. Even in-context learning with gold logical examples yields $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp (n.s.). Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$).
115	
116	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%).} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning. When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
117	
118	Four supporting analyses confirm the mechanism: constraint ablation shows MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 94.4\% of predictions ($n = 804$); 91.8\% of constraints are discriminative but mirror the majority; effects concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+12.8$\,pp) with direction determined by accuracy regime; and hyperparameter peaks coincide with low SC baselines (Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}).
119	
120	\begin{figure}[t]
121	\centering
122	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}
123	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--60\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}
124	\label{fig:regime-scatter}
125	\end{figure}
126	
127	
128	\subsection{Remediation: 25+ Strategies Fail}
129	\label{sec:remediation}
130	\label{sec:interventions}
131	
132	We test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
133	
134	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\% to 6.0\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
135	
136	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\Delta = -9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\ref{app:remediation}). Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles) produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
137	
138	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All three families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, forming a same-model trade-off ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.85\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) suggests weaker models suffer more severe confirmation bias; all models exceed $1.0$, a pattern that persists from 7B open-source to frontier closed-source (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help: $C_0 < 5\%$ implies degeneration, SC $\geq 60\%$ implies confirmation bias, SC $\leq 40\%$ implies error amplification. Whether trained extractors shift the observed trade-off boundary remains open.
139

144	\label{tab:kappa_generalization}

$p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\\sim$50--60\\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 50.00 & 51.50 & $+$1.50 & 0.581 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 55.88 & 59.31 & $+$3.43 & 0.092 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 54.41 & 53.92 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%)}} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 74.00 & 71.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 75.49 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 79.00 & 78.00 & $-$1.00 & 0.625 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 83.33 & 84.80 & $+$1.47 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.83 & 85.67 & $-$0.17 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Degenerate ($C_0 < 5\\%$)}} \\\\\nQwQ-32B & AIME 2024$^\\dagger$ & 22 & 72.70 & 72.70 & 0.00 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\\dagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold logical annotations + Z3)}} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & \\textbf{89.71} & $\\mathbf{+13.73}$ & $2.0{\\times}10^{-7}${***} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 74.00 & \\textbf{91.17} & $\\mathbf{+17.17}$ & $9.21{\\times}10^{-15}${***} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. Two conditions are jointly necessary for any positive trend: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}).\n\nThis correlation manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts logical constraints consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.85\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 79.00 & $-$1.00 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 83.33 & $+$1.47 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.98 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 50.00 & $+$1.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence limitation: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Effects}\n\\label{sec:main-results}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nSICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime-scatter}).\n\n\\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\\sim$50--60\\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\\,pp. The default-seed and seed-456 runs share identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), suggesting that seed-level constraint quality, not SC level alone, influences the effect.\n\n\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%).} All nine Type~A conditions produce no significant positive effects. Even in-context learning with gold logical examples yields $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp (n.s.). Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$).\n\n\\paragraph{Type C (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%).} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\\%$) shows $\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \\emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.\n\nFour supporting analyses confirm the mechanism: constraint ablation shows MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 94.4\\% of predictions ($n = 804$); 91.8\\% of constraints are discriminative but mirror the majority; effects concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+12.8$\\,pp) with direction determined by accuracy regime; and hyperparameter peaks coincide with low SC baselines (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}\n\\caption{SICA $\\Delta$ (pp) vs.\\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \\geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--60\\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}\n\\label{fig:regime-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation: 25+ Strategies Fail}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}). Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles) produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, forming a same-model trade-off ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\\times$ Mistral $> 3.85\\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\\times$ LLaMA) suggests weaker models suffer more severe confirmation bias; all models exceed $1.0$, a pattern that persists from 7B open-source to frontier closed-source (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help: $C_0 < 5\\%$ implies degeneration, SC $\\geq 60\\%$ implies confirmation bias, SC $\\leq 40\\%$ implies error amplification. Whether trained extractors shift the observed trade-off boundary remains open.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. All families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$, confirming an architecture-independent ceiling. $\\kappa$: Fleiss' kappa on raw answers.}\n\\label{tab:kappa_generalization}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Aggregation & $\\kappa$ & Eff-$K$ & $K$ & Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & Debate & 0.15 & 4.44 & 12 & 38.24 \\\\\n& Diverse-Prompt & 0.40 & 2.21 & 12 & 54.41 \\\\\n& Self-Consistency & 0.44 & 2.02 & 10 & 56.86 \\\\\n& ICG & 0.47 & 1.93 & 10 & 51.96 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & Self-Consistency & 0.41 & 2.17 & 12 & 66.18 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & Self-Consistency & 0.66 & 1.46 & 12 & 75.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & Self-Consistency & 0.74 & 1.32 & 12 & 75.98 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Test-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.
4	The dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.
5	SC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.
6	\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.
7	The gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.
8	
9	Confidence-weighted \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this, but the core limitation persists.
10	Recent neuro-symbolic methods \citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, luo2025logicagent} report gains on logical reasoning but introduce components beyond the reasoning model.
11	A simpler question precedes these: can the \emph{same} model extract useful formal constraints from its own traces---a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model or data?
12	No prior work has systematically evaluated this.
13	
14	We ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \citep{austensmith1996}.
15	When independence fails \citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.
16	Measuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding $K_{\text{eff}}$ as an ordinal diagnostic of vote diversity ($K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.2$--$3.6$ for nominal $K = 12$; \S\ref{sec:condorcet}).
17	Chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.46$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
18	When the same model extracts logical constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} (SCB): on problems where the model errs, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratios $1.66$--$4.97\times$ across nine models), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
19	A shuffle control shows this bias is structural: it reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.
20	Self-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.
21	
22	We study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
23	Across six open-source models (7B--32B), three domains (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations with four-seed replication, twenty-seven training-free remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all produce neutral or negative results (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}).
24	Prior work shows LLMs struggle with natural-language self-correction \citep{huang2024selfcorrect} and that LLM-generated logical formulas can be unreliable \citep{stechly2025selfverification}. Our formal channel is provably sound---the failure is precisely localizable to the extraction step. The assumption that formalizing reasoning provides \emph{independent} verification evidence fails not because Z3 is unreliable (99\% of constraints are syntactically valid), but because extraction inherits the model's biases: MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems, rendering the formal layer vacuous.
25	
26	Oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\,pp Mistral-7B, $+13.73$\,pp Qwen-14B on FOLIO, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}), yet self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of this ceiling.
27	The central finding is a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off \citep{krogh1994neural}: Debate reduces $\kappa$ to 0.15 but collapses accuracy ($-18.6$\,pp); other methods cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling across three families (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
28	A cross-architecture proof-of-concept escapes the bottleneck: zero-shot DeBERTa NLI yields $+6.67$\,pp on Mistral-7B ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$) but no significant gain for stronger generators; domain-adapted NLI amplifies gains to $+$12--26\,pp ($p < 0.0001$), extending to generators with SC up to 72\% (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
29	
30	Our contributions are:
31	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
32	\item \textbf{Quantifying symbolic confirmation bias in formal self-verification.} While the general difficulty of LLM self-correction is known \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}, we provide the first quantitative characterization of symbolic confirmation bias in formal self-verification, demonstrating that provably sound verification machinery becomes informationally vacuous under self-extraction: 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong answer (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$ across nine models), yet 99\% pass Z3 validation. A shuffle control and MAX-SAT ablation show this bias is structural---the formal layer adds no information beyond majority voting. Twenty-seven training-free strategies fail to break it; cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
33	
34	\item \textbf{General diagnostic framework for trace-aggregation methods.} Three diagnostics ($K_\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly predict success or failure across all 60 tested conditions spanning self-extraction, training-free remediation, cross-architecture verification, and oracle controls. Decision boundaries---$C_0 < 5\%$ (degenerate), $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ (diversity-limited), $\text{BR} > 2\times$ (bias-dominated), $\kappa_\text{same} \geq 0.40$ (same-model ceiling)---provide zero-cost prediction of symbolic aggregation viability. The framework extends to any method extracting structured representations from LLM traces (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
35	
36	\item \textbf{Process-level independence analysis.} CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$) across four models from three families; under cross-model extraction on frontier models, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; \S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
37	\end{enumerate}
38	
39	\begin{figure}[t]
40	\centering
41	\small
42	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tikzpicture}[
43	    node distance=0.3cm and 0.1cm,
44	    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.55cm,
45	                text width=1.2cm, align=center, font=\scriptsize},

```text
1	\section{Background \& Setup}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}
5	\label{sec:condorcet}
6	
7	Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority convergence under independent voters \citep{austensmith1996}, but LLM traces violate this independence. Following \citet{ladha1992correlated} and \citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:
8	\begin{equation}
9	\label{eq:effk}
10	K_{\text{eff}} = K \,/\, [1 + (K{-}1) \cdot \kappa].
11	\end{equation} In our experiments (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ yields $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.2$--$3.6$ for $K{=}12$, serving as an ordinal diagnostic of vote diversity rather than a literal effective sample size. This independence limitation motivates our investigation.
12	
13	\subsection{SICA Pipeline}
14	\label{sec:pipeline}
15	
16	Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\{t_1, \ldots, t_K\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.
19	
20	\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts logical constraints from each trace at $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}); in practice, 99.0\% of extracted constraints are propositional at the Z3 level, with only 1.0\% using true quantifiers. The \emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.
21	
22	\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence (global average deduplication ratio: 46.0\%); each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:
23	\begin{equation}
24	\label{eq:maxsat}
25	\mathcal{C}^* = \arg\max_{\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{C}} \sum_{c \in \mathcal{S}} w_c \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \mathcal{S} \text{ is satisfiable.}
26	\end{equation}
27	Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\texttt{answer\_a} == \texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.
28	
29	\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus} = \mathcal{C} \setminus \mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:
30	\begin{equation}
31	\label{eq:score}
32	\text{score}(a) = \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^*} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a) - \alpha \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a),
33	\end{equation}
34	where $\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \arg\max_{a} \text{score}(a)$.
35	
36	\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:
37	\begin{equation}
38	\label{eq:degenerate}
39	\text{score}(a) \approx \sum_{\substack{c \in \mathcal{C}^* \\ \text{source}(c) \to a}} w_c,
40	\end{equation}
41	equivalent to majority voting. We show in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.
42	
43	\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\{q : |\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\}| / |\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic: $C_0 < 5\%$ means SICA reduces to SC. $\alpha = 0.5$ is not critical (${\pm}0.49$\,pp across $\alpha \in [0, 1]$).
44	
45	\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}
46	\label{sec:mirage}
47	
48	We hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}). Unlike general self-correction failure \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}, the symbolic mirage is specific to formal verification: the solver is provably sound, yet its outputs are informationally equivalent to majority voting because self-extracted inputs carry the model's distributional prior. We quantify mirage intensity via the \emph{Bias Ratio}:
49	\begin{equation}
50	\label{eq:mirage}
51	\text{BR}(M,T) = \frac{P(c \text{ supports } a_M \mid a_M \neq a^*)}{P(c \text{ supports } a^* \mid a_M \neq a^*)},
52	\end{equation}
53	where $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\text{eff}} < 2$ (\emph{direct}; Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). If traces are unfaithful \citep{turpin2024language}, the mirage is inherent to extraction. Two observations motivate the hypothesis: (O1)~BR $> 1.0$ across all tested self-extraction conditions (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (O2)~no training-free remediation reduces BR below $1.0$ (\S\ref{sec:remediation})---both initially observed on Mistral-7B and subsequently confirmed across nine models. These lead to a testable prediction: (P1)~structurally independent verification should break SCB (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
54	
55	\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=gray!5, colframe=gray!50, boxrule=0.3pt, left=3pt, right=3pt, top=1pt, bottom=1pt, before skip=4pt, after skip=4pt]
56	\textbf{Empirical Finding 1} (Self-Extraction Ceiling).\label{prop:ceiling}
57	For generator $M$ with $\text{BR}(M) > 1$ and oracle improvement $\Delta_\text{oracle}$, self-extracted constraints improve SC by at most $\Delta_\text{max}^\text{self}$, empirically bounded by $\Delta_\text{oracle} / \text{BR}(M)$.
58	\end{tcolorbox}
59	
60	\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=gray!5, colframe=gray!50, boxrule=0.3pt, left=3pt, right=3pt, top=1pt, bottom=1pt, before skip=4pt, after skip=4pt]
61	\textbf{Empirical Finding 2} (Capability Gap Condition).\label{cor:capability_gap}
62	Let $G$ be a generator with SC accuracy $\text{SC}_G$ and let $V$ be an external verifier with standalone accuracy $V_\text{acc}$ and inter-system correlation $\rho_{GV} < \rho_{GG}$. Combo gain $\Delta_\text{combo} - \Delta_\text{SC}$ decreases monotonically with $\text{SC}_G$, with significant gains observed up to $\text{SC}_G \approx 70\%$.
63	\end{tcolorbox}
64	
65	\textit{Empirical support.} From Finding~1, adding $V$ with $\rho_{GV} < \rho_{GG}$ increases $K_\text{eff}^\text{combo}$. Empirically, combo gain decreases monotonically with $\text{SC}_G$ ($\rho = 1.0$, $p = 0.003$, $n{=}6$; \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}), with significant improvements even when $\text{SC}_G > V_\text{acc}$.
66	
67	We also define a Conditional Correction Ratio (CCR, Appendix~\ref{app:cil}) that summarizes these diagnostics.

      "content": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority convergence under independent voters \\citep{austensmith1996}, but LLM traces violate this independence. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = K \\,/\\, [1 + (K{-}1) \\cdot \\kappa].\n\\end{equation} In our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ yields $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2$--$3.6$ for $K{=}12$, serving as an ordinal diagnostic of vote diversity rather than a literal effective sample size. This independence limitation motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts logical constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}); in practice, 99.0\\% of extracted constraints are propositional at the Z3 level, with only 1.0\\% using true quantifiers. The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence (global average deduplication ratio: 46.0\\%); each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic: $C_0 < 5\\%$ means SICA reduces to SC. $\\alpha = 0.5$ is not critical (${\\pm}0.49$\\,pp across $\\alpha \\in [0, 1]$).\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}). Unlike general self-correction failure \\citep{huang2024selfcorrect}, the symbolic mirage is specific to formal verification: the solver is provably sound, yet its outputs are informationally equivalent to majority voting because self-extracted inputs carry the model's distributional prior. We quantify mirage intensity via the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). If traces are unfaithful \\citep{turpin2024language}, the mirage is inherent to extraction. Two observations motivate the hypothesis: (O1)~BR $> 1.0$ across all tested self-extraction conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (O2)~no training-free remediation reduces BR below $1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation})---both initially observed on Mistral-7B and subsequently confirmed across nine models. These lead to a testable prediction: (P1)~structurally independent verification should break SCB (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=gray!5, colframe=gray!50, boxrule=0.3pt, left=3pt, right=3pt, top=1pt, bottom=1pt, before skip=4pt, after skip=4pt]\n\\textbf{Empirical Finding 1} (Self-Extraction Ceiling).\\label{prop:ceiling}\nFor generator $M$ with $\\text{BR}(M) > 1$ and oracle improvement $\\Delta_\\text{oracle}$, self-extracted constraints improve SC by at most $\\Delta_\\text{max}^\\text{self}$, empirically bounded by $\\Delta_\\text{oracle} / \\text{BR}(M)$.\n\\end{tcolorbox}\n\n\\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=gray!5, colframe=gray!50, boxrule=0.3pt, left=3pt, right=3pt, top=1pt, bottom=1pt, before skip=4pt, after skip=4pt]\n\\textbf{Empirical Finding 2} (Capability Gap Condition).\\label{cor:capability_gap}\nLet $G$ be a generator with SC accuracy $\\text{SC}_G$ and let $V$ be an external verifier with standalone accuracy $V_\\text{acc}$ and inter-system correlation $\\rho_{GV} < \\rho_{GG}$. Combo gain $\\Delta_\\text{combo} - \\Delta_\\text{SC}$ decreases monotonically with $\\text{SC}_G$, with significant gains observed up to $\\text{SC}_G \\approx 70\\%$.\n\\end{tcolorbox}\n\n\\textit{Empirical support.} From Finding~1, adding $V$ with $\\rho_{GV} < \\rho_{GG}$ increases $K_\\text{eff}^\\text{combo}$. Empirically, combo gain decreases monotonically with $\\text{SC}_G$ ($\\rho = 1.0$, $p = 0.003$, $n{=}6$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}), with significant improvements even when $\\text{SC}_G > V_\\text{acc}$.\n\nWe also define a Conditional Correction Ratio (CCR, Appendix~\\ref{app:cil}) that summarizes these diagnostics.\n",

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	\paragraph{Method.}
6	\looseness=-1 We test with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs as entailment, contradiction, or neutral. In combo mode, each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times alongside SC's $K$ votes. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$).
7	
8	\paragraph{Main result.}
9	\looseness=-1 DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo $w{=}3$ yields $+2.94$\,pp (n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n{=}600$), combo $w{=}3$ yields a significant gain only for Mistral-7B ($+6.67$\,pp, $p < 0.001$); all generators with SC $\geq 72\%$ show no significant improvement ($\Delta \leq +0.83$\,pp, $p > 0.4$). Same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). After BH correction ($m{=}12$), two conditions remain significant (Appendix~\ref{app:capability-gap}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}
12	\looseness=-1 Four generators spanning a 47-point SC range on ProofWriter ($n{=}600$), all paired with DeBERTa-large-mnli (55.00\% standalone; Table~\ref{tab:cross_generator_nli}): only Mistral-7B (SC 39.33\%, below the verifier) gains significantly ($+6.67$\,pp, $p < 0.001$); all three generators above the verifier (SC $\geq 72\%$) show no significant improvement ($\Delta \leq +0.83$\,pp). Per-label analysis confirms complementary error patterns that diminish as the generator improves (Appendix~\ref{app:multi-nli}). The monotonic relationship holds across three NLI architectures and six verifier-generator pairs (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$, $p = 0.003$, $n{=}6$; Figure~\ref{fig:capability-gap}; Appendix~\ref{app:capability-gap}).
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\small
17	\caption{Cross-generator NLI escape with DeBERTa-large-mnli verifier (55.00\% standalone, ProofWriter $n{=}600$). Combo gain decreases monotonically with generator capability. Only Mistral-7B (SC below the verifier) shows a significant gain.}
18	\label{tab:cross_generator_nli}
19	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
20	\toprule
21	Generator & Family & SC & Gap & Combo $\Delta$ ($w{=}3$) & $p$ \\
22	\midrule
23	Mistral-7B & Mistral & 39.33\% & $+$15.67 & $+$6.67\,pp & $<$0.001 \\
24	LLaMA-3.1-8B & LLaMA & 72.50\% & $-$17.50 & $+$0.83\,pp & 0.665 \\
25	Qwen2.5-14B & Qwen & 74.00\% & $-$19.00 & $+$0.67\,pp & 0.503 \\
26	Qwen3-14B & Qwen & 85.83\% & $-$30.83 & $+$0.50\,pp & 0.45 \\
27	\bottomrule
28	\end{tabular}}
29	\end{table}
30	
31	\paragraph{Domain-adapted verifier.}
32	\looseness=-1 Fine-tuning DeBERTa on ProofWriter depth-5 OWA (99.67\% standalone) amplifies the escape: combo gains reach $+$12.67\,pp (Mistral-7B, $w{=}3$) and $+$11.67\,pp (LLaMA-8B)---a $14\times$ amplification over LLaMA's zero-shot result ($+0.83 \to +11.67$\,pp)---with near-total error complementarity ($364{:}2$ and $164{:}1$; Table~\ref{tab:finetuned-nli}, Appendix). A cost-aware cascade recovers 83--90\% of full-combo gain at ${\sim}35\%$ NLI cost (Appendix~\ref{app:drc}).
33

1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\looseness=-1 We test the three predictions of the symbolic mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}).
5	
6	\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}
7	\label{sec:effective-k}
8	
9	\looseness=-1 Table~\ref{tab:independence} (Appendix) reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$ as an ordinal diagnostic: higher $K_{\text{eff}}$ indicates greater vote diversity, though it should not be interpreted as a literal effective sample size (\S\ref{sec:condorcet}). Across four Mistral-7B seeds, $\kappa \in [0.41, 0.51]$ yet $\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp, indicating seed-level constraint quality matters beyond independence. Positive $\Delta$ requires both sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ degrades). All four model families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}).
10	
11	
12	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
13	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
14	
15	\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\% of errors overlap with SC. A shuffle control reveals this bias is \emph{structural}: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\text{BR}_\text{null} \approx \text{BR}_\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error. Z3 rejects only 3.4\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems on Mistral-7B FOLIO (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\geq$99\% across $\alpha \in \{0.1, \ldots, 0.9\}$ for both high- and moderate-BR conditions (Table~\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).
16	
17	\looseness=-1 This pattern generalizes: $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms SCB universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.
18	
19	\begin{table}[t]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
23	\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\text{err} \approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\kappa_{\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}
24	\label{tab:frontier-scb}
25	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}
26	\toprule
27	Model & Ext. & SC (\%) & $n_\text{err}$ & BR & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
28	\midrule
29	Mistral-7B & $^a$ & 55.88 & 90 & 4.97 & 0.00 \\
30	LLaMA-8B & $^a$ & \textrm{---} & \textrm{---} & 2.88 & \textrm{---} \\
31	Qwen-14B & $^a$ & 75.98 & 49 & 3.85 & $-$0.49 \\
32	\addlinespace
33	R1-Distill-8B & $^b$ & 74.51 & 52 & 2.92 & $+$2.45 \\
34	gpt-4o & $^a$ & 73.91 & 53 & 2.94 & $-$1.80 \\
35	gpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\
36	gpt-5.5 & $^b$ & 84.31 & 32 & 3.48 & $+$0.49 \\
37	o3 & $^b$ & 82.65 & 35 & 1.66 & 0.00 \\
38	Gemini-2.5-Pro$^*$ & $^b$ & 87.37 & 12 & 2.79 & $-$1.10 \\
39	\bottomrule
40	\end{tabular}}
41	\end{table}
42	
43	\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\text{BR} = 18.8\times$ versus $2.7\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). SCB reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.
44	
45	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
46	\label{sec:process-kappa}
47	
48	\looseness=-1 We quantify reasoning-process overlap via ProcessSim, the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace vectors (Eq.~\ref{eq:process-sim}, Appendix~\ref{app:process-gradient}). ProcessSim captures lexical overlap; deeper structure measures may reveal additional patterns, so we treat it as supporting rather than primary evidence. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean ProcessSim $= 0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.46$: traces share reasoning processes even when answers diverge. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}), from $0.31$ (LLaMA) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with ProcessSim ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\ref{tab:process-gradient}), modulated by self-bias severity.
49	
50	
51	CCR analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields $\text{CCR} > 0$ while same-model SICA yields $\text{CCR} < 0$, with CCR correlating with combo gain ($\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\ref{app:cil}).
52	
53	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
54	\label{sec:diagnostics}
55	\label{sec:remediation}
56	\label{sec:interventions}
57	
58	\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). Four representative failure modes illuminate the independence-accuracy trade-off. \emph{Debate} ($\kappa = 0.15$, $\Delta = -18.6$\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy---same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\kappa$ slightly but $\Delta \approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. The \emph{oracle ceiling} ($+29.90$\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality. All non-debate strategies cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling across three families. No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-individual}).
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}
63	\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\Delta$ vs.\ SC. Grey band: $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
64	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\paragraph{Prospective held-out validation.}
68	\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\%$, $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $K_\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\Delta \leq 0$); the observed $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp confirms the prediction.
69	
70	\paragraph{Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis: verification.}
71	\looseness=-1 The observations and prediction of \S\ref{sec:mirage} are confirmed. Observations O1--O2, initially discovered on Mistral-7B, generalize: BR $> 1$ across three self-extraction models, six cross-model pairs, and all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); 27 training-free strategies fail to reduce BR below 1.0 (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). Prediction P1 is validated: cross-architecture NLI escapes the mirage for generators up to SC $\approx 70\%$ (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
72

1	\enlargethispage{14\baselineskip}
2	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	\label{sec:conclusion}
5	
6	\looseness=-1
7	The three diagnostics ($K_\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) extend beyond SICA to any trace-aggregation method, correctly classifying all 60 tested conditions. Decision boundaries generalize prospectively to a held-out model family (\S\ref{sec:diagnostics}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-discussion}), enabling practitioners to predict viability from a pilot run ($K{=}12$) at zero cost.
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	SCB applies to any method extracting structured representations---self-verification \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}, self-refinement \citep{madaan2023selfrefine}, chain-of-verification \citep{dhuliawala2023chainofverification} all face the same distributional prior. Escaping requires (i)~\emph{structural independence}---the verifier must not share architecture or training data with the generator; (ii)~\emph{capability ordering}---combo gain decreases monotonically with generator SC; zero-shot NLI benefits only the weakest generator, while domain-adapted NLI extends gains to generators with SC up to 72\%; (iii)~\emph{task-verifier alignment} (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}). For test-time scaling \citep{chen2025sets, lifshitz2025mav}, with $K_\text{eff} \leq 3.6$ for $K{=}12$, same-model traces scale redundancy, not information---effective scaling requires structurally independent verifiers or trained signals escaping the model's prior.
11	
```
